How Trump's Undermining of the Constitution Helped Cause a "Blue Tsunami"

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Mostly law professors | Sometimes contrarian | Often libertarian | Always independent

Trump's unconstitutional policies on tariffs, the Iran War, and immigration played a big role in his party's enormous "Blue Tsunami" election defeat.

On Tuesday, Trump and the Republican suffered a massive electoral defeat, losing control of both houses of Congress. Many have called it a "Blue Tsunami". The defeat was caused by multiple factors. But Trump's unconstitutional actions on tariffs, the War in Iran, and immigration played a major role. Violations of the Constitution don't always result in political setbacks for the perpetrators. But in this case they did "bigly." As Texas GOP Senator Ted Cruz put it, "Tariffs have made everything too expensive, and the Iran war has dragged on too long."

This connection is perhaps most obvious in the case of tariffs. Since returning to power in January 2025, Trump has engaged in a series of massive tariff power grabs, repeatedly attempting to usurp congressional authority in this field, and use it to impose the highest tariff schedule since the Great Depression. He started with the IEEPA tariffs. When the Supreme Court invalidated them, he tried again with Section 122 and Section 301 of the Trade Act of 1974, and the Section 338 tariffs against Canada. In each case, he illegally tried to transform relatively narrow delegations to the executive into a blank check to impose tariffs at will. The net effect of these actions has been to raise prices for consumers,  reduce economic growth, and impose some $700 billion in new taxes on Americans over a 10-year period (unless courts end up striking down the new tariffs, too). The tariffs also failed to produce the promised increase manufacturing employment.

Most relevant for present purposes, affordability and price increases were clearly a huge issue in the election. And most voters have come to realize that tariffs increase prices, and damage the economy. Exit polls reveal that 66% of voters believe Trump's tariffs have hurt the national economy (compared to only 21% who believe they helped), and a similar percentage (64%) believe they hurt the economy in their area (only 14% believe they helped it). Numerous other polls show widespread opposition to the tariffs, as well (e.g. here and here). They became a major issue in the elections, linked to the broader issues of price increases and "affordability."

Polls, pundits, and even Donald Trump and Vice President J.D. Vance all agree the Iran War was a major factor in GOP losses. The military stalemate combined with resulting energy price increases have enraged voters. Exit polls showed that 74% of voters said the war was a "crucial" or "important" factor in their decisions, and the vast majority of them disapproved of Trump's conduct of it.

And, as I have argued from the beginning, that war is unconstitutional because it was never authorized by Congress.  As I warned at the start of the conflict, the failure to build up the public supported needed to secure congressional authorization greatly reduced the odds of success:

This [constitutional] limitation on presidential power is more than just a technical legal point. The requirement of congressional authorization for the initiation of war is there to ensure that no one person can take the country to war on his own, and that any major military actions have broad public support, which can be essential to ensuring that we have the will and commitment needed to achieve victory against difficult opponents. Trump's failure to seek and secure that kind of broad public support has ensured that only about 27 percent of Americans approve of this military action, compared to 43 percent who disapprove, according to a Reuters poll. Other surveys show similar results. This is a historically low level of public support at the start of a major military action and bodes ill for U.S. staying power if we suffer reverses or a prolonged conflict results.

I think that prediction has (so far at least) been vindicated by events.

The immigration situation is somewhat more complicated than that with tariffs and war. But, here too, various unconstitutional actions have damaged the GOP's political standing and hurt the party in the election. Trump's cruel mass deportation policies have made ICE by far the most the most unpopular federal agency (surpassing even the IRS) and alienated Hispanic voters in Texas and elsewhere;  this was a key factor in the Republicans' defeat in that state. GOP immigration policies  led many Hispanics who backed Trump and the GOP in 2024 to turn against them in 2026.

In part because of indefensible double standards in relevant Supreme Court precedent, courts permit a wide range of harsh and cruel conduct in immigration policy, that would be invalidated in any other context. But many of Trump's policies went beyond even these overly permissive bounds. As a result, the administration has lost many thousands of immigration detention and deportation cases, including many on constitutional grounds, and many before GOP-appointed judges. Trump's immigration crackdown has violated the Constitution in various other ways, as well, as in the case of his use of an absurdly broad definition of "invasion" in order to justify detaining and deporting even legal migrants without due process.

In all three of these cases, voter backlash arose primarily because of the awful - and highly visible - practical consequences, rather than because of the constitutional issues themselves. Most voters care far more about military failure, rising prices, and visible cruelty of these policies than about their legality. Nonetheless, it is still true that unconstitutional conduct played a major role in causing the tsunami that swept away many GOP officeholders.

We should not be overly optimistic about this chain of events.  It would be a mistake to assume that unconstitutional policies always lead to electoral punishment, or even usually do so. In many cases, voters are unaware of illegality or don't give it much weight if they do find out. Previous presidents have often avoided significant political backlash for violating the Constitution. Barack Obama, for example, also waged two unconstitutional wars (those against Libya and ISIS). But because the wars were (at least initially) militarily successful, led to few casualties, and had little visible negative economic impact, he largely avoided political damage. Later, Obama concluded that the Libya war was his worst mistake. But Democrats didn't pay much, if any, political price for it.

Trump's unconstitutional actions on these three issues turned out be politically costly because the negative effects were large and highly visible. Even many generally ignorant and inattentive voters noticed them.

Here, as always, I do not claim that public support for my position somehow proves I was right. As the author of Democracy and Political Ignorance: Why Smaller Government is Smarter, I recognize that majority public opinion is heavily influenced by ignorance and bias, and thus often a poor guide to goo policy. Moreover, while majority opinion agrees with me in condemning Trump's policies here, it would not go as far as I would in sweeping away nearly all tariffs and immigration restrictions.

Despite these caveats, I am glad that the public punished the GOP for these terrible unconstitutional policies. I hope this outcome might deter future presidents from imitating them. At the very least, they might hesitate to pursue them on the same massive scale as Trump did.

NEXT: May Court Order Parent to "Immediately Cease All Disparagement of" Other Parent "via Social Media"?

Ilya Somin is Visiting Professor of Law at the University of Virginia, Professor of Law at George Mason University, Simon Chair in Constitutional Studies at the Cato Institute, and author of Free to Move: Foot Voting, Migration, and Political Freedom, Democracy and Political Ignorance: Why Smaller Government is Smarter, and The Grasping Hand: Kelo v. City of New London and the Limits of Eminent Domain.

The Canadian Bacon In A McDonald's Egg McMuffin Isn't Slow-Brined

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Along with the fact that the McDonald's Egg McMuffin is endlessly customizable (even the simple addition of extra butter upgrades the sandwich), its base ingredients make for a bite that is a symphony of rich, savory flavors and includes enough variety of textures to keep your mouth interested until the very end. One of the most compelling components of the once good, now great McMuffin is the Canadian bacon: the thin, disc-shaped meat that seemingly has more in common with ham than the crispy strips we associate with bacon. It might interest you to know that this tender slab of pork doesn't get its salty flavor from a slow brine, or really, any immersive procedure at all. Instead, the brine is actually injected into the meat during the manufacturing process, according to Food Tech Today (per YouTube).

McDonald's hasn't publicly released which of its partners actually produces its Canadian bacon, but it's as good a guess as any that it could be Lopez Foods, a long-time known beef supplier for the burger chain. Whoever manufactures the Canadian bacon, though, does so by following a highly mechanized process, where the pork loins, fresh from the slaughterhouse, go through a series of machines in the plant. One of these machines contains hundreds of needles that automatically inject the brine, made mostly of salt, sugar, sodium nitrate, and food coloring, into the trimmed but still largely unprocessed pork as it travels down the production line. The brine is necessary for flavor, as well as texture, to ensure the meat stays tender and juicy.

The journey those pork loins make from the moment they enter the processing plant until they end up as slices on a McDonald's McMuffin is actually fascinating, and no part is more interesting than the second half of the operation. This is where the pork is packed tightly into sleeves, steamed for around five hours until fully cooked, and the brine has the opportunity to permeate and distribute all throughout the meat. Then, the tubes of pork are sliced into exact discs by a machine and placed in its vacuum-sealed packaging. Instead of arriving frozen at McDonald's locations, as the sausage does, the Canadian bacon arrives merely refrigerated.

To cook the Canadian bacon and give it the requisite browning that appears on the finished McMuffin, employees simply grab a number of the slices and place them down in the front of the clamshell grills, so that they're not under the lid. They cook for eight seconds on each side, and unlike McDonald's burgers, which don't get flipped, an employee must manually turn them over. The precise timing allows the slices to warm through, but not cook for so long that they crisp up, which could then be a hop, skip, and a jump away from burning and wasting Canadian bacon.

Tracksmith Created the Blueprint for Indie Running Brands. Can It Survive Without Its Architect?

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The Boston-born apparel company enters a new era following the sudden departure of founder Matt Taylor.

We earn a commission for products purchased through some links in this article. Why Trust Us?

The running journalist Mario Fraioli still remembers the shorts Matt Taylor sent him in 2014, when Taylor was launching a new apparel brand.

Dubbed the Longfellow short, the prototype pair had a button at the waist, they were longer than typical running shorts, and they didn’t have a liner. The idea was, in Fraioli’s telling, that you could go straight from an easy run to a coffee shop and still look presentable. Taylor wanted to know what Fraioli thought.

Fraioli’s reaction? He didn’t love them. The sizing was off for him.

He was, however, intrigued by Taylor’s passion for the apparel and the nascent brand.

“He was super excited,” Fraioli told Runner’s World.

With $35,000 of his savings from a previous endeavor and seed round capital, Taylor launched Tracksmith later in 2014. The initial product line was four men’s items, including those Longfellow shorts and a “Grayboy” T-shirt that mimicked the standard-issue shirt of college athletic departments, plus a spike bag and Meter magazine. Women’s clothing followed about nine months later.

The idea of Tracksmith? Top-quality running apparel for people who were not professional athletes, but trained hard and loved their sport. The names and designs of the products reflected the roots of the sport and Taylor’s reverence for its history.

Frugal runners initially had sticker shock. Tracksmith manufactured its clothing in the U.S., using quality materials, which cost more. But the company was one of the earliest independent running brands selling apparel for serious runners outside of the major shoe companies. Women’s running apparel brand Oiselle had hatched earlier, in 2007.

Tracksmith quickly grew. It added retail locations in Boston and New York, and hundreds of items for sale—including four shoe styles, which were added in 2022.

Now, Taylor has been abruptly pushed out of the company he founded, and industry observers are left wondering if the brand can keep growing without him.

Fraioli, who is now a coach and writer of the Morning Shakeout newsletter, announced the news of Taylor’s departure in his October 13 email, writing that the newsletter would no longer have Tracksmith as a sponsor.

“A big part of my belief in Tracksmith was tied to my belief in Matt’s character, authenticity, and creative vision, and with him gone, I’ve lost confidence in where the brand is headed,” he wrote.

It’s unclear what precipitated the split. Taylor declined to speak to Runner’s World, and former employees were in the dark about the details of his ouster.

Taylor had hired a CEO in January, Jared Carver, a veteran of Converse, to handle the day-to-day operations so he could get back to focusing on the “brand’s vision and creative expression,” according to a company LinkedIn post eight months ago.

In a concurrent post, Taylor wrote: “I didn’t start Tracksmith to be a CEO—I started it to change the way running is presented with the hope of changing how it is perceived. My goals were to build distinct products, tell compelling stories and create a sense of camaraderie, the same you might feel on your cross country team.”

Insiders and one analyst painted a picture of a brand that opened a lane for rival independent retailers to follow suit—and perhaps surpass—Tracksmith. Bandit and Satisfy are two of the brands frequently cited as competitors, especially as the sport has seen enormous growth coming out of COVID.

“Other people have, I think, drafted on their success and made the marketplace more competitive,” said Matt Powell, an industry analyst who is a senior advisor with BCE Consulting. “For any brand, I think it’s hard to stay ahead of the trends, and you never have an unassailable position. Somebody’s always trying to copy you, or do what you do better. That's a big challenge for a small brand.”

It is not known if Tracksmith was profitable, as the company is privately held, and those who were close to Taylor in the company’s early days wondered whether investors and board members were growing impatient. Tracksmith would not talk about the company’s financials or name the members of its board. A Tracksmith representative confirmed Taylor’s exit and that of Matt Pauk, the head of product, who left in August, and offered a statement:

“Matt Taylor, Tracksmith’s founder, has transitioned out of his role with the company and departed Tracksmith. Matt’s unique vision created the Tracksmith we know today, and undoubtedly shaped the culture and business of running. At its core, Tracksmith has always been about people—our team, our partners, the people who push the sport forward, and the runners we serve. These values remain unchanged, and we are confident in our ability to continue building upon the strong foundation established in Tracksmith's 12 years.”

As early employees try to process what happened, they expressed appreciation for what Taylor brought to the industry.

Taylor, they said, was the animating force of the brand. He named the various products after runners in history, and arranged artful photo shoots with athletes in the mist and the mud. His love of the sport was obvious. (He’s still a serious runner. In 2024, Taylor ran the Boston Marathon, at age 47, in 2:43:18.)

Lou Serafini was one of the first employees. Like Fraioli, he didn’t immediately get what the brand was about. At the time, he was working at Heartbreak Hill Running Company before Taylor recruited him to work for Tracksmith.

“I remember being kind of confused,” Serafini said, “not really understanding what they were trying to do. I think most visionary brands have a little bit of that, where it has to be different enough where it makes people kind of scratch their head a little bit.”

Serafini went to Tracksmith’s first Boston Marathon pop-up in 2015, in an empty storefront at 285 Newbury Street, which in 2017 became its permanent home, just blocks from the marathon finish line. (Now pop-ups are a standard part of major marathons with cutting-edge brands; expos are in dreary convention centers, a necessary stop for runners to make to pick up their bibs.)

Serafini’s first job was managing the shop and building a running community there. “That was the first time where I really realized the ethos of the brand,” he said.

When he first started at Tracksmith in 2017, if Serafini saw any runners wearing Tracksmith clothing along the Charles River paths, it was an exciting moment. By the end of his tenure, in 2024, he remembered spotting runners in the brand all over the world.

The best days at Tracksmith always have been Boston Marathon Monday and the Tuesday after. Runners would crowd the shop, pick up a free poster, and get it stamped with their finishing time. “The focus of those days was to sit in the track house and stamp posters and have a 30-second conversation with somebody about how their race went,” Serafini said. In 2018, the year Boston went off in a nor’easter, the Tracksmith headquarters on Newbury became a hospital of sorts for runners trying to get warm.

Two other former employees, in conversations with Runner’s World, echoed Serafini’s assessment of Taylor as a visionary.

One former employee, who asked not to be named, said the committed runner didn’t have much to choose from before Tracksmith, describing the industry’s 2014 offerings as “neon, boring, and not high performance.” Tracksmith took the better products the pros were getting and gave them to amateurs. (And though the price point was initially surprising, these days, in the era of $300 super shoes, it seems reasonable.)

But getting eyeballs on a new business is cash intensive, and Taylor had big, expensive operations, which included stipends for unsponsored Olympic Marathon Trials qualifiers and hosting twilight 5Ks for the fast amateurs looking to run PRs. Real estate on Newbury Street and in Brooklyn does not come cheap, either. A London location that opened in 2023 closed in August.

Without Taylor, does the brand have legs? The fear is that it becomes “performance J.Crew,” preppy running apparel without the investment in the sport.

In response to a question about bolstering the sport, the Tracksmith spokesperson confirmed the company would be continuing with its programming and support of amateur runners through clubs and meets like Tracksmith Stamata, the Twilight series, Hare AC, and the Amateur Support Program. “Nothing has changed or will change there,” the spokesperson said.

But with Taylor’s departure, some see Tracksmith being at a crossroads, caught between funders seeking a return on their investment and fast-growing competition.

“I think a lot of investors, private equity investors, they revere founders and understand that the business that they’re in, particularly one like this, it’s as much of a cultural business as anything else,” said Ryan Eckel, who worked for Tracksmith as the brand president for five years, from 2019 to 2024. “And when you start treating it like it’s making widgets, I think you lose sight [of the culture] and ultimately I think it hurts their investments long term.”

Powell, though, still sees a lane for Tracksmith, especially with consumers who are willing to pay premium prices.

“On one level, I would tell you there's never been a better time to be a small brand, because I think the consumer is looking for unique products,” he said. “But because the consumer is always demanding new and next, that part is really hard for a small brand to execute.”

Sarah Lorge Butler is a writer and editor living in Eugene, Oregon, and her stories about the sport, its trends, and fascinating individuals have appeared in Runner’s World since 2005. She is the author of two popular fitness books, Run Your Butt Off! and Walk Your Butt Off!

Ads In Google AI Answers Concentrate In Apparel & Electronics via @sejournal, @MattGSouthern

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

This webinar uses Conductor's research to help you prioritize marketing data and AI visibility measurement fixes.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

This webinar uses Conductor's research to help you prioritize marketing data and AI visibility measurement fixes.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Webinar: 9 AI Workflows For Better Content. Nov 18, 2 PM ET.

New holiday data counts ads inside Google AI Overviews, AI Mode and ChatGPT across five shopping categories and shows which prompts ChatGPT ads ran on.

Adthena reported that approximately 175,100 paid ad placements were found within Google’s AI Overview panel as well as AI Mode panels inside the U.S. during the period of June to September. This included five holiday shopping categories; however, the majority of these paid placements belonged to apparel and consumer electronics.

The Holiday AI Paid Search Report also counts 204,489 ChatGPT ad placements in the same five categories over the same months. Adthena sells ad tracking for Google and ChatGPT, and the report closes with an offer for a free trial of its ChatGPT Ads product.

Some U.S. apparel and electronics advertisers may already be using portions of their existing campaigns to serve ads to users who see AI Overviews and AI Mode. The report identifies where the ads appeared, but does not track which ads were clicked, how much was spent, nor what conversions were achieved on either surface.

The total number of ads in Google’s AI Answers (in the U.S.) in this report is based upon a combined total for both AI Overviews and AI Mode. In that count, apparel has approximately 111,000 placements; consumer electronics have about 55,000; travel has roughly 6,500; beauty has about 2,100; and toys and games have around 685.

There are no separate ad buys for these placement types. According to Google’s Help Page regarding Ads in AI-Powered Experiences, all Search, Shopping, and Performance Max campaigns that exist are automatically eligible to appear in AI-Answers. Although advertisers cannot specifically target only AI Overviews or AI Mode, or opt out of those placements apart from Search, they may still utilize negative keyword targeting and/or brand exclusions.

The report also reviewed how many times AI Overviews appeared in Google Results for both August of 2025 and August of 2026. Going from 304 million to 575 million, U.S. appearances saw an 89% increase over that period. The UK/EU saw an increase of 160%, while Australia saw an increase of 536%. The report doesn’t describe the queries behind those counts or say whether the set stayed the same across both years.

The report counts 204,489 ChatGPT Ads placements across five categories, Apparel, Consumer Electronics, Travel, Toys & Games and Beauty, from June through September 2026.

Adthena’s press release frames it more broadly, stating more than 200,000 advertisement placements occurred since OpenAI started allowing others to buy advertising space on the chatbot.

In the report’s count, apparel accounted for the largest number of advertisement placements at 90,517. Next was Consumer Electronics with 56,569; then Travel with 48,134; followed by Toys and Games with 4,694 and lastly Beauty with 4,575.

In addition, the company reported that there were 1,082 different advertisers in those five categories using the service in June, 3,741 advertisers in July, and 6,333 advertisers in August. The company projected the number of advertisers in September.

According to the report, the advertisers in its data in Toys and Games increased the most during that time frame from June to August, increasing 13 fold, from what the company calls a relatively low base.

The report states that the total number of advertiser placements included in their research covered all markets, but does not identify which markets or state whether they continued to monitor the same market(s)/queries throughout the entire four-month period. In addition, OpenAI expanded its footprint during that time.

On August 18, OpenAI announced that ChatGPT Ads would be available in 31 European countries. The company began offering this service one week later. Additionally, on September 23, OpenAI began rolling out ChatGPT Ads to seven additional Asian markets.

For perspective, on October 21 OpenAI stated that more than 100,000 advertisers have run campaigns on ChatGPT Ads since the advertising capability was opened up to outside parties just over eight months earlier. This figure represents all categories and the complete period of time that ChatGPT Ads has been open to third-party advertisers, so it isn’t directly comparable to the report’s monthly counts.

In addition to that, the report groups ChatGPT ads based on the nature of the search that an ad was run against. It describes research and discovery queries as “people comparing or asking what’s best rather than naming a product.”

In beauty, 83% of ads ran on those queries, while travel and toys & games were at 69% and 67% respectively. Apparel and consumer electronics came in under half, at 48% and 42% respectively.

Again, the press release references only the data related to beauty, travel and toys. The report provides no information about how questions were categorized.

Since Google includes searches that prompt ads in AI Overviews and AI Mode in aggregate in its standard reports for all of Search, they are intermingled throughout your search terms report.

In ChatGPT, best-of and comparison prompts carried most of the observed ads in beauty, travel & toys, and less than 50% of those seen in apparel and electronics.

After the report’s time frame closed, Google’s ads within their AI answers continued to evolve. Google announced that they were launching multi-turn ads in the United States on October 28. These allow advertisers’ ads to show up on subsequent searches (queries) in AI Mode. In addition, Google stated that ads in AI Mode are scaling to ten additional countries including Canada and Australia. They further stated that Brazil, Mexico and the United Kingdom were planned for Q4 2026.

OpenAI’s October 21 announcement expanded ChatGPT Ads to 89 countries. The most recent Adthena data is from September so does not include these new developments nor the weeks surrounding Black Friday.

May Court Order Parent to "Immediately Cease All Disparagement of" Other Parent "via Social Media"?

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Mostly law professors | Sometimes contrarian | Often libertarian | Always independent

An appellate court orders a trial court to consider whether such an order is consistent with the First Amendment.

From McWilliams v. Meghrian, decided Monday by Georgia Court of Appeals Judges Jeffrey Watkins, Christopher McFadden, and J. Wade Padgett:

This appeal arises from a child custody modification proceeding in which the trial court, [among other things,] … permanently enjoined … Mother … from making any "disparaging" statements about Father "via social media or other means" and modified child support. Mother appeals, and for the reasons below, we vacate the trial court's final order and remand for further proceedings. The parties are the parents of one child, born in 2008….

Mother argues that the final order and accompanying parenting plan included unconstitutional prior restraints on speech. The final order directed Mother to "immediately cease all disparagement of [Father] whether via social media or other means." The final order also adopted and incorporated the parenting plan proposed by Father, which provided that the parties "will not speak badly of each other or the members of the family of the other parent." …

During the final hearing, the trial court stated, "So, Ms. McWilliams, when we're dealing with, you know, parents—and I understand what you said. You're putting your truth out there and saying your truth. Disparagement doesn't care about the truth. It just means that you're putting down another parent." Mother responded, "Yeah." The court concluded:

And so publicly like that is something that you just—you can't do, and so we want to be careful about that. There's a reason that we're careful about that. Things on social media get back to kids. They just do. And I think that we're all—we're all cognizant of that in this day and age, but we have to be careful about what we put out there. And if we can't say anything nice, we just don't need to say anything at all, and that's the best rule to live by in terms of this. So that's—that's kind of what we need to—what we need to work on there….

As to Mother's failure to assert her constitutional challenge before the trial court, it is true that "[b]ecause this Court is a court for the correction of errors, we will not consider matters, even of constitutional magnitude, that were not raised and ruled upon in the trial court." However, the injunction, in its objectionable form, was not entered until the final order. And Mother raised the issue in her application for discretionary review, which was her first opportunity to do so.

Accordingly, on remand, the trial court is instructed to address the constitutional issue in the first instance. As we do not reach the constitutional issue, we need not decide whether, as Father argues, it is moot because the child has reached 18 years of age…. In sum, we vacate the grant of attorney fees, the injunction (both in the final order and in the incorporated parenting plan) restricting speech, and the order of child support….

Malone Williams Allen represents the mother.

Eugene Volokh is the Thomas M. Siebel Senior Fellow at the Hoover Institution at Stanford, and the Gary T. Schwartz Distinguished Professor of Law Emeritus and Distinguished Research Professor at UCLA School of Law. Naturally, his posts here (like the opinions of the other bloggers) are his own, and not endorsed by any institution. He is also the co-host of the Free Speech Unmuted podcast.

Curiosity captures a martian dawn

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The morning Sun casts a hazy glow over Mars’ Mount Sharp, lighting up distant wind-carved rock formations known as yardangs. This scene is cropped from a six-image panorama captured by the Mastcam on NASA’s Curiosity rover at 8:30 a.m. local time on Aug. 11, 2026, the mission’s 4,982nd sol (martian day). Mount Sharp, formally Aeolis Mons, stands 3.4 miles (5.5 km) tall at the center of Mars’ 96-mile-wide (154 km) Gale Crater. Since 2014, Curiosity has climbed roughly 0.6 mile (1 km) up its stratified slopes, reading each new layer like a chapter of martian geology.

The mountain’s lowest, oldest layers likely formed from sediment deposited by lakes and streams that have long since vanished. This photo is the most detailed view to date of the much younger yardang layer. Of particular interest is the fact that the color and orientation of the yardangs don’t match the surrounding material. Scientists think these features could have been built up from volcanic ash that settled after a nearby eruption, and was then carved away by the wind. Curiosity should reach the yardangs’ base in 2027, where its instruments will help uncover how the layer formed.

Brooks Mendenhall is a staff writer at Astronomy, based in Chattanooga, Tennessee, fueled by an unending curiosity about the universe. A former classroom teacher, he has a knack for breaking down complex concepts for a wide audience.

Interstellar Visitor 3I/ATLAS Shows Water Ice Behaves the Same Across Alien Star Systems

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Targets of opportunity sometimes happen in astronomy, and we got a major one when the third interstellar object, 3I/ATLAS, was discovered in July 2025. And it just so happens that in the intervening years between the last known interstellar visitor (2I/Borisov in 2019), we got a major upgrade in our observational capabilities with the launch of the James Webb Space Telescope (JWST). A new paper from a team of researchers led by Dr. Silvia Protopapa of NASAâs Goddard Space Flight Center and the Southwest Research Institute, and published in The Astrophysical Journal Letters, details JWSTâs findings on the water ice content of this most recent visitor from beyond our solar system.

Typically, water ice is pretty easy to spot for astronomers. In infrared, which is JWSTâs specialty, it absorbs light very clearly at 1.5, 2.0, and 3.0 microns in wavelength. Weâve known that for years, and have seen evidence of it in regular comets based strictly on our solar system, such as Hartley 2.

But when JWST looked at 3I/ATLAS, it clearly showed the 3.0 micron absorption line, but the 1.5 and 2.0 ones were surprisingly weak or absent. It also featured a blue near-infrared continuum slope across 1.15â2.50 microns that wasnât typical of the other comets zooming around our solar system. These discrepancies led some observers to question whether 3I/ATLAS actually contained any water ice at all, but the new paper with higher resolution observations quickly put paid to that idea.

Fraser talks about the orbital mechanics of 3I/ATLAS

To prove that it does, indeed, contain water ice, the research team modeled how light scatters through âmixedâ particles that contain both water and dust. This lined up much more closely with the observed spectral lines of 3I/ATLAS, proving that, instead of pure water ice with a clear pattern, the ice is locked up into microscopic grains that blend water and dark rocky minerals, such as pyroxene and carbon.

Light can only travel so far through these aggregates before hitting some dust, causing a shortened optical pathway. That, in turn, mutes the absorption features at the 1.5 and 2.0 micron level, while the longer 3.0 micron wavelength is still able to pass straight through. The tiny grains also scatter shorter wavelengths more effectively, which explains the general blue hue that JWST saw.

In fact, JWST observed this interstellar visitor at three different times during its journey through our solar system, starting when it was about 3.31 astronomical units (AU) out from perihelion all the way through when it was about 5.65 AU past perihelion. And the differences in 3I/ATLASâs spectra were noticeable at each stage. After perihelion, the grains of ice and dust appeared to include a second population of larger, micron-sized, ice-rich aggregates.

Fraser talks about what would happen if we could catch an interstellar object.

Also, after perihelion the researchers noticed a new optical effect known as a Fresnel reflection near 3.1 microns, which indicated that there was also âcrystallineâ water ice in 3I/ATLAS. This differs from the normal, disorganized type of ice normally seen, as its water molecules arrange themselves in neat crystalline lattices rather than in a jumble. According to the paper, these layers were only visible after perihelion either because the Sun itself warmed the surface layers causing them to reconfigure into a crystal, or the solar energy caused the outer dust to blast away, revealing cleaner underlying crystalline structures that were previously hidden.

But perhaps the most interesting part of the paper was the parallels it was able to draw with our own solar system. The infrared patterns of 3I/ATLAS match certain types of Trans-Neptunian Objects (TNOs) in our own solar system. These balls of ice and rock are the planetary leftovers that float out beyond Neptune in the Kuiper belt, and JWST has already spent plenty of time staring at them. Previously, researchers had seen the same 3.0 micron absorption pattern without the 1.5 and 2.0 micron features, but had thought those patterns might be attributable to complex hydrocarbons or organics. Turns out it might just have been dusty ice all along, at least according to the new paper.

Itâs unclear whether there will ever be another opportunity like this in JWSTâs operational lifetime. But thereâs still plenty left to understand about our most recent interstellar visitor. As more and more scientists continue to analyze the data that was collected during 3I/ATLASâs sojourn through our system, thereâs undoubtedly more to be discovered about our latest interstellar visitor, and especially how it can explain more about our own solar system.

Goodbye, GrubHub. Amazon Prime Has a New Meal Delivery Partner: Uber Eats

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

The switch comes with a limited-time offer.

Your Amazon Prime membership has a new perk — and it’s available now.

On Thursday, Amazon announced that Prime members in the US will get free delivery on Uber Eats orders made directly through Amazon’s app or website. And you’ll get $20 off the first two Uber Eats eligible orders of $25 or more for the first 14 days, plus Amazon-exclusive offers. If you have a Prime Visa credit card, you’ll earn 5% back on all Uber Eats orders until Dec. 5, with a Prime membership.

It’s another perk that may make Amazon Prime membership worthwhile, especially if you already use Uber Eats. But you may wonder what that means for the GrubHub partnership that both Amazon and GrubHub touted for years — dating back to 2022. Before now, your Prime subscription included a GrubHub Plus membership at no additional cost. GrubHub Plus included no delivery fees, lower service fees and 5% back on pickup orders.

The new partnership launch just so happens to be the same day that Lyft and DoorDash announced that they’re teaming up for a new subscription, Lyft Go. An Amazon spokesperson said in an email to CNET that the GrubHub and Amazon partnership ended on Thursday. There’s no telling how long Amazon will partner with Uber Eats.

“We continuously evaluate our third-party offers to ensure they provide the best possible value for Prime members,” the spokesperson said. “As part of that ongoing evaluation, we identified an opportunity to deliver an even better restaurant delivery experience.”

However, a GrubHub spokesperson said in an email to CNET that if you have a Prime membership and activated GrubHub Plus, you’ll have the membership until Dec. 31, 2027.

GrubHub shared a joint statement from Amazon and GrubHub in an email to CNET:

On Nov. 5, Grubhub and Amazon have decided to end the Prime-exclusive Grubhub Plus offer, and Amazon customers will no longer be able to place Grubhub orders through Amazon.com or the Amazon Shopping app. Amazon and Grubhub are proud of the partnership and the value provided to Prime members over the years. As the agreement concludes, both companies remain committed to serving our customers and delivering a great customer experience.

The new Uber Eats perk is now available to all Prime members, including those with Prime Access and Prime for Young Adults. And if you’re looking for another dining option, your Amazon Prime membership still offers free same-day delivery on perishable grocery orders over $25 when you order from Amazon Grocery.

Sheffield Wednesday face Oldham Athletic in the FA Cup on Sunday as Dave Challinor takes charge of his new side for the first time. The new SWFC boss has a squad full of talented players to pick from, but he has injuries to consider too.

And, speaking to the press ahead of his maiden game, Challinor confirmed that trio Liam Cooper, Joe Lumley and Ali Al-Hamadi are set to remain out. Cooper is more of a precautionary decision, while Lumley and Al-Hamadi are targeting returns to training next week.

In better news, Di’Shon Bernard is fit and available having trained all week. Challinor and the Owls will remain cautious with his load management after a long time on the sidelines.

If he already has a good idea of his favoured XI, he can take this game to give chances to those projected to be on the fringes to get a clearer picture of the full squad at his disposal.

Lumley’s continued absence means Ashley Maynard-Brewer will likely keep his place in goal. Cooper being on the sidelines gives Bernard a good chance of coming straight into the side as well.

Pair this with the youthful exuberance of Ernie Weaver, Will Grainger and Harry Gray, and SWFC have a squad built for the short and long-term. There’s a good balance of profiles and characters, making up a recipe for success at Hillsborough.

Challinor faces plenty of selection headaches in deciding his best lineup, and he will be excited to find it out.

Longtime investor Martin Tobias says no to 99% of startups — his new AI tool helps them find a yes

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Chronicling the Seattle and Pacific Northwest startup scene. SEE MORE

by Kurt Schlosser on Nov 5, 2026 at 7:01 amNovember 5, 2026 at 7:01 am

Martin Tobias was pitched by about 4,500 startups last year. He wrote 10 checks.

Now the pre-seed investor is offering the rest of them a consolation prize: an AI tool that tells founders which investors are most likely to say yes.

Tobias, a longtime figure in the Seattle tech scene, is now based in Spokane, Wash. He founded Loudeye, a digital media company that went public during the dot-com boom and later sold to Nokia, and went on to lead biofuel maker Imperium Renewables and group-buying startup Tippr.

He was a venture partner at Ignition Partners, an early backer of DocuSign, before starting Incisive Ventures in 2020. The firm closed a $10.5 million fund last year to back early-stage B2B software companies.

Investor Match started as an internal tool to help Incisive’s portfolio companies find seed and Series A investors. Tobias said he has 17,000 contacts but can only keep about 150 of them in his head, a limit known as Dunbar’s number.

To use the tool, founders upload a pitch deck, and Investor Match returns 20 investors ranked by fit, based on stage, check size, geography and what each firm has actually backed. It also drafts outreach emails, but it doesn’t send them.

Most founders send those emails cold, or pass the list to their existing investors and ask for warm introductions, Tobias said. Investors who have accounts on the platform can also respond to founders directly.

A free preview shows all 20 matches with fit scores, with the top three fully expanded. The full list costs $20, which Tobias said roughly covers the API costs of running it, about $1,000 a month.

Every founder Tobias turns down now gets pointed to the tool.

“Just because I wasn’t the right investor for that company doesn’t mean that the right investor wasn’t out there,” he told GeekWire.

Incisive appears on the platform like any other investor. Some founders who were matched with the firm asked whether Tobias had tilted the algorithm toward himself. He said he hadn’t, and that he only wants pitches that actually fit.

“Just because I wasn’t the right investor for that company doesn’t mean that the right investor wasn’t out there.”

Other companies offer investor matching as part of broader fundraising platforms, including Metal and OpenVC, which Tobias said charge founders monthly subscriptions. He said OpenVC relies on simple keyword matching, while Investor Match uses AI to read each investor’s website, portfolio and blog posts, which takes “millions and millions of tokens.”

Some of those companies are trying to build venture-scale businesses off founders’ fundraising, Tobias said.

“I think that’s just f—ing evil,” he said. “I don’t think you should try to make money off people who quit their job and are putting everything into trying to raise their first one to $2 million.”

Founders have told Tobias they’ve raised more than $100 million from investors they found through the platform since it launched about six months ago, though he acknowledged he can’t verify that because the money doesn’t flow through Investor Match.

Investor Match is also an extension of his investing thesis. He said his fund looks for B2B software companies like DocuSign that “reduce friction at scale” by taking a business process and doing it better in software.

“That’s exactly what Investor Match is,” he said. “What’s the friction in the market that you’re dealing with? Investors finding founders, founders finding investors. OK, can software help with that? I think it can.”

Swallowed by the Camino Green Tunnel

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The room we slept in was the top floor of a centuries-old, three-story building with a modern interior. Tuck-pointed right under the roof, we slept soundly through the night as periodic thunder and a heavy rain provided peaceful white noise overhead.

Stepping outside the next morning, sunrise illuminated a surreal sky, backlighting the massive monastery. We paused at a local cafe for a quick breakfast before leaving Samos behind.

Our hike started with a few miles of road walk before the Camino veered onto a forested, ancient track. Thick rock walls lined the path, guarded by groves of massive chestnut trees.

As the morning sun broke through, the air naturally turned humid. We began a steady climb along a narrow path where pilgrims over the years had woven stick crosses directly into the fencing alongside the trail.

Highlight of the Day: We ran into Anthony from Oxford–who was walking his fifth Camino. He explained that this sunken section of path is called a Holloway. Worn deep into the earth over centuries of foot traffic, it creates a stunning green tunnel with 12-foot mossy walls and a dense canopy of ancient trees overhead.

Our “tramily” friends, Jill and Robert, caught up with us midmorning, and the miles flew by as we chatted. We meandered from one quaint hamlet to the next along quite country roads. By 10:30 AM, we only had 2.5 miles left for the day.

We soon reached Foxos, a tiny village with cute medieval farm houses, old barns, and a historic church. From there, the path climbed steeply toward Aguiada, where our trail merged back on the main Camino Frances route. We were completely out of water, but this small village didn’t have any water sources available.

Reconnecting with the main route brought a dramatic shift. Because you only need to walk the final 100 km to earn a Compostela certificate, and since it was the start of the weekend, the trail was suddenly packed with weekend hikers and new pilgrims starting their journey from Sarria.

It instantly felt like a different Camino–less intimate, much more crowded, and distinctly touristy.

We rolled into Sarria at 1:00 PM. Built into a hillside, Sarria is a charming medieval city full of narrow, winding cobblestone streets.

Our accommodation was easily one of our favorites so far:

While Knockers immediately crashed for a two-hour nap, I got to work planning our journey past Santiago.

Next Steps: We officially decided that after reaching Santiago, we will hike out to Finisterre—traditionally known as “the end of the world” on the Atlantic coast. From there, we’ll return to Santiago and head south along the Portuguese Caminos. (There are a few ferry connections and complex logistics to map out for that stretch!)

Later that evening, we walked downhill to a local grocery store and definitely bought too much food. Being hungry and finding fresh, real ingredients for the first time in a long while made it impossible to resist.

Back at the apartment, we cooked up a huge meal and ate until we couldn’t take another bite. By the time “hiker midnight” rolled around, we crashed hard for another peaceful night’s rest.

That started an idea of a longer term forest bath on the AT. What a long strange trip its been. Any financial donations I get will be used to send out love to other thru hikers a trail magic.

SHT Day 14: The Nearly Unfortunate Incident

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Last night was my final night spent in my tent. For the remainder of this year’s SHT adventure I will be sleeping in my camper. I heard coyotes at one point in the early evening, and also a train all night long. It didn’t blare its horn all the time, but I definitely heard the constant hum of the motor.

I knew I had shorter mileage today, with my husband likely arriving later in the afternoon, so I was yet again in no hurry to leave camp. I stretched out and took off around 9:20. The morning was a quiet, lonely hike, passing by Beaver Pond and Fault Line campsites without seeing anyone.

The farther north I go, the more the trees have changed color.

I had planned to unpack my stove and take a legitimate coffee break at Fault Line, but opted instead to just shake up the Trader Joe’s instant coffee with cold water when I filtered up at a stream. I do love a hot coffee on a cold morning, but I can be kinda lazy and I prefer not to stop for long once I’m in a groove. I definitely could have fired up the stove before I left camp, but I already had my breakfast shake. Besides, having this “cold brew” mid-morning was a nice jolt and change from plain water.

The trail did some steep ups and downs through what seemed like prime moose habitat before descending into the town of Beaver Bay. I crossed Lax Lake Road and kept going. I had service and talked to Bill (the husband), who said his ETA would just about match mine at the trailhead outside the next town of Silver Bay, another three or four trail miles for me.

This is one of my favorite pics. It would be top ten if there was a moose down there!

After Lax Lake Road I started to see more people. I passed by the Beaver River campsites, the second of which would have been an amazing spot to stay. Instead, I had L&L here, and stayed just long enough to appreciate its beauty. The latrine seemed private enough, as it was set back in the woods, away from the river and tent spots. Just after I finished my business, I heard an ATV coming closer. Turns out there was a trail, mere feet behind the latrine! I finished in the nick of time!

The driver didn’t seem to notice me, or at least knew the trail well enough to expect someone may be on the throne and look away. This is the second time I’ve noticed a latrine well within sight of passers-by. The first was at East Gooseberry Campsite, where it was I who happened to see an unfortunate person doing their business on the latrine, as it was not very hidden at all. I screeched a quick “I’m so sorry!” as I looked away, feeling terrible for the person caught with their literal pants down. Let’s just say some latrine spots out here are better than others.

Day hikers took my picture on the Beaver River bridge.

The view from near one of the Beaver River campsites.

After the close call, I headed on. The trail crossed a few roads and came very close to the golf course in Silver Bay. It also briefly followed the Gitchi-Gami trail, a well-established and paved trail for non-motorized use. There were a few final steep climbs and descents over rocky cliffs when I suddenly saw Bill on trail! He came up to meet me about a quarter mile from the Penn Boulevard trailhead. We finished the steep downhill together and crossed Penn Boulevard to reach the massive trailhead, where the sweet sight of our truck and camper was waiting for me.

The Gitchi-Gami crossing under the train tracks.

Bill had an electrolyte drink ready for me, and I stretched while we talked trail news. This trailhead, the largest I’ve seen for the SHT so far, is just on the outskirts of Silver Bay. It must be popular!

Now we’re off to Lamb’s Resort, near the town of Schroeder, for the next few days. This campground will serve as our first home base as I continue to tick off trail miles as a series of day hikes. Having completed nearly 100 miles so far, I’m ready for a zero day, a shower, and a bed with a snuggly electric blanket.

How To Optimize For Better Campaign Leads, Not Just Conversions – Ask A PPC via @sejournal, @navahf

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

This webinar uses Conductor's research to help you prioritize marketing data and AI visibility measurement fixes.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

This webinar uses Conductor's research to help you prioritize marketing data and AI visibility measurement fixes.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Webinar: 9 AI Workflows For Better Content. Nov 18, 2 PM ET.

Improve lead quality by feeding qualification and revenue data back to ad platforms, so automated bidding can prioritize business outcomes over form fills.

This month, a really good question came in:

“My campaigns are generating plenty of leads and hitting our CPA target, but the sales team says the quality is getting worse. How can I optimize for better leads rather than just more conversions?”

The short answer is: If you want better leads, you first need to teach the platform what a better lead looks like.

It’s very easy to lose track of real-world performance when platform-reported performance looks great. Your CPA may be within target and lead volume may look healthy, while your sales team is telling you those leads aren’t turning into meaningful opportunities.

Those two things aren’t necessarily contradictory.

The ad platform may actually be doing exactly what you asked it to do: finding more of the conversions you told it were valuable. If every form fill is treated as the same conversion equally valuable, the bidding system doesn’t inherently know that one became a strong sales opportunity and another went nowhere.

That means improving lead quality starts with the definition of success you’re giving the platform.

In this post, we’ll look at how to define a better outcome, pass that information back to the platform, and give automated bidding enough meaningful signal and time to optimize toward it.

A quick disclosure: I am a Microsoft Advertising employee, and while I’ll reference Microsoft Advertising capabilities where they’re useful, these principles can apply to paid media programs across platforms.

Before changing your bidding strategy or implementing a new measurement tool, ask a more fundamental question:

What actually makes one lead better than another for your business?

For some advertisers, the initial form fill may be meaningful enough. For others, a more granular segmentation might be needed:

The goal should be to get as close as you reasonably can to the deepest meaningful funnel outcome you can reliably measure.

If 100 leads came through but only 10 were SQLs, you don’t want to teach the bidding system that all 100 were equally valuable simply because they completed the same form.

The closer your conversion tracking gets to the outcome the business actually cares about, the more meaningful the information you’re giving the bidding system.

That’s also why marketing and sales alignment matters here. If the platform says CPA is great while Sales says lead quality is deteriorating, both things can be true.

Before optimizing for “better leads,” make sure everyone agrees on what better actually means. Check that your Sales team is using current lead quality to inform their decisions rather than applying historical bias, as lead quality can change and improve over time with campaign optimizations.

See also: The Conversion Setup Errors That Break Smart Bidding

Once you’ve defined the outcome, the next step is giving the platform information that helps distinguish higher-value actions from lower-value ones.

This is where real-world qualification and revenue data become particularly useful. The best signal any ad platform can work off of is conversion values, and passing accurate values will ensure budgets are directed effectively. This is why conversion value rules are so effective: by adapting the conversion value based on probability of value, you can make it even easier for ad platforms to deliver the right leads.

If you aren’t going to use real-world numbers or outcomes, your CPA or ROAS goals may need to be treated as directional guidance rather than representations of actual business results.

There will also be cases where your ultimate outcome doesn’t happen frequently enough to give conversion-based bidding sufficient volume and signal to effectively optimize performance.

That’s where an earlier but still meaningful conversion can help. A lead becoming marketing-qualified, sales-qualified, or progressing further into your funnel tells you more about lead quality than an unrelated engagement metric.

A useful benchmark for automated bidding is at least 30 conversions within 30 days. Following significant changes to budgets or targets, conversion-based bidding can take as many as roughly 50 conversions to calibrate according to multiple platforms.

See also: Ask A PPC: What Are Learning Periods In Digital Marketing?

Once you know which outcomes matter, you need a way to pass that information back to the platform.

If you can’t yet measure true value, identify the most meaningful signal you can track reliably and use conversion value rules to assign it directional value. If you’re able to measure and optimize against real value, use offline conversion imports to bring that data into your ads platform. This can fill in the gaps so platforms optimize against real value.

That’s where mechanisms such as offline conversions and Conversions APIs come in.

Offline conversions let you connect outcomes that happen after the immediate website experience back to the original ad interaction. Think about someone who clicks an ad, fills out a form, talks to a sales contact, and eventually becomes a customer.

Microsoft Advertising, for example, documents the Click ID, conversion time, and conversion goal name among the core information used for offline conversion imports.

And Conversions API, commonly called CAPI, lets conversion information move programmatically from your systems into the ad platform rather than relying exclusively on periodic manual uploads.

Microsoft Advertising’s Conversions API lets advertisers send conversion and engagement events directly from their servers using Universal Event Tracking (UET), and can pass business signals including revenue, product details, click IDs, hashed identifiers, and consent status.

The mechanism isn’t the strategy. The quality of the signal you’re sending through it is.

Before automating the flow of conversion data, make sure you trust the data you’re automating.

If your CRM is treating poor-quality leads as successful outcomes, passing that information back faster won’t solve the problem. You’ll simply create a more efficient feedback loop around the wrong definition of success.

This also applies to gaps in volume or value of conversions. Advertisers should confirm where the gap is appearing:

Auditing for these factors, as much as the technical passing through of conversion data, is a critical step in closing the loop.

See also: Is Your Conversion Data Misleading You? Seven Common Google Ads Tracking Issues

There is one more piece that can easily get overlooked: conversion delay.

Your campaign may look one way when you initially review results and very different after enough time has passed for leads to move through the sales process.

Microsoft Advertising conversion tracking supports configurable conversion windows, depending on the goal, from one minute up to 90 days.

CAPI introduces another consideration. On Microsoft Advertising, CAPI currently has a 90-day look-back window. Consider which outcomes realistically happen soon enough to pass back through that mechanism.

Your systems still need time for sales to work the lead or for a customer to actually convert.

Your reporting cadence should reflect your actual sales cycle, and automated bidding needs enough meaningful data and time to learn.

That may mean resisting the instinct to make aggressive changes because yesterday’s platform numbers don’t yet contain outcomes that take weeks to materialize.

Everything above assumes the campaign’s purpose is ultimately a conversion-driven business outcome.

If you’re running an awareness or consideration campaign, it may generate sales and booked demos, but these should be reported as secondary key performance indicators rather than the appropriate immediate measures of success. Instead of forcing every campaign into the same CPA or ROAS framework, start by being explicit about the business outcome the campaign is intended to drive and select measurement signals appropriate to that objective.

The important thing is consistency between what you want the campaign to accomplish and what you’re asking the platform to optimize toward.

Conversion totals also don’t explain everything happening between an ad interaction and the eventual outcome.

That’s where diagnostic signals can be useful.

Search behavior can help you understand whether you’re creating interest. Search term reporting, for example, can surface changes in branded searches or the kinds of queries coming from your audience.

Microsoft Clarity can help diagnose what happens after the click. Behavioral analytics such as recordings, heatmaps, and engagement insights from your website can help surface whether visitors are meaningfully exploring the site, encountering friction, or leaving without taking the intended action. Clarity AI Visibility can add another discovery signal by showing when content is cited in supported AI-generated experiences and which AI grounding queries triggered those citations.

These signals aren’t substitutes for your business outcome.

Instead, use them to help answer questions the conversion number alone can’t: Are we creating interest? Where are we losing it? Is campaign performance the problem, or is something happening later in the customer journey?

If you want better leads, don’t just ask your ad platform for more conversions. Teach it what a better conversion looks like.

Start by defining the deepest meaningful outcome you can reliably measure. Then pass qualification, revenue, or other value signals back where appropriate, so the platform has a better understanding of what success means to your business.

Use offline conversions, CAPI, or other feedback mechanisms to connect those real-world outcomes with advertising activity. And make sure you’re giving automated bidding enough meaningful signal and enough time to learn from it.

If you choose not to pass those real-world outcomes back, that’s valid too. Just be honest about what your platform metrics represent and keep your internal source of truth at the center of your business decisions.

The platform can only optimize toward the definition of success you give it.

So when lead volume looks great, but quality doesn’t, don’t immediately ask the bidding system to find more.

First, make sure you’ve taught it what “better” means.

Les Tells Designs Handbags Around the Body, Not Just the Outfit

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Les Tells founder Brianna Tell’s work is best explained by the intersections that inspire it. There’s the influence of her hometown of Lancaster, Pennsylvania, and her professional experience in Milan, Italy. There’s the intersection of experimental design and generations-old Italian craftsmanship. But perhaps the most important intersection is the one between bag and body. Where rigid corners and bulky silhouettes can cause discomfort, Tell wanted to explore what physical cohesion between bag and body could look like—a piece that functions not simply as an accessory, but as an extension of the wearer’s silhouette.

The result is the first collection from Les Tells: six luxury Italian-made bags and accessories designed to fit and complement the wearer’s body. Released on Nov. 3, the collection features four bags, a wallet, and a wristlet designed to follow the body’s natural curves. The idea was that an accessory could be luxurious and distinctive without the discomfort and awkwardness that can accompany high-concept design.

The idea came to Tell while she was visiting Paris. “I was watching all of these beautiful French people walking down the street as they are, and I just saw all of them in these beautiful outfits,” Tell recalls. “But then I saw some of the bags that they were wearing, and it just felt like they were sticking out from their body. That was the moment that I was like, why aren’t there bags that move better with your body?”

From there, she began the design process, sketching out silhouettes for crossbody bags and handbags. The concepts were distinctive, but the true test would come in how they moved with the body.

Tell’s approach to testing is, admittedly, a bit unconventional. Knowing the importance of how the pieces worked with a body in motion, she constructed paper models, testing out the bags’ mobility and identifying points of friction. The approach had its initial skeptics.

“I showed up with these paper models and [my friend] was like, ‘Nobody’s going to take you seriously. This is something that is not professional,'” she recalls. “But I still believed in this, and I wanted to keep on going. I went down and talked to those ateliers.”

She found a collaborator in a family-run atelier in Scandicci, near Florence. “They looked at me like I built something so different and unique that it really verified my gut feeling that I was on the right track.”

Over two years, she refined the designs. The Alexandras, a cylindrical bag with a detachable strap, has an inward curve designed to nestle comfortably against the wearer’s hip. The Logans, featuring an asymmetrical, oblong silhouette, is a crossbody bag designed to sit comfortably under the arm or along the curve of the back. The Kathleens, a shoulder bag, features a gentle curve that doesn’t compromise its interior capacity. Each design follows the same principle: structure without sacrificing comfort.

The decision to use Italian leather was inspired by Tell’s upbringing near horse farms in Pennsylvania, where leather was a familiar material, as well as her time studying in Florence. The hardware and jewelry were designed alongside the bags, echoing their forms and creating a cohesive finish. Offered in four colorways, Tell wanted to create a collection that felt both personal and universal—one designed to be worn across a range of genders and body types.

“In the campaign photos, there are different body types; there are different ages; there are different people,” Tell explains. “They get to decide if they want to wear that bag. It’s not me that says, ‘Okay, this is just for a woman.'”

Tell’s focus has remained squarely on design, down to the collection’s less visible details. To improve comfort and functionality, the “Les Tells” embossed tab on the Alexandras acts as a latch for the magnetic clasp that secures the flap. The Kathleens feature a hidden strap extender tucked inside the bag, plus a microfiber case for glasses. After two years of meticulous development, Tell believes the attention to detail has paid off. After all, you only get one chance to launch your first collection.

Tell has already started work on the next iteration, including a new set of the paper models that raised eyebrows when she began developing the debut collection. This time, however, she’s confident in the process that brought her designs to life. “I’ve had this gut feeling for so long that people are really going to appreciate the bags,” she says. “And I’m really ready to show the world.”

The Les Tells collection is now available at lestellsworld.com, with prices starting at $350.

An all star cast and leading director aren’t enough to distract eagle eyed critics.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The 2026 Christmas advert season is here, but with so many brands throwing their hat in the ring, standing out requires some real ingenuity. This year, brands have embraced a handcrafted approach, like Levi's stunning stop-motion film and Prada's Disney-inspired ad, but it seems there's an imposter among the ranks.

Dior's latest holiday ad has been lambasted for allegedly using AI-generated content, disguised as the work of human craft. While the best Christmas adverts don't follow a formula, Dior's 'animated' ad appears to be stolen valour, proving that audiences are hungry for authenticity when it comes to advertising.

Directed by French animator Sylvain Chomet, “Christian Dior’s Night in Color,” is (on the surface) a charming short film inspired by the founder's friendship with film star Marlene Dietrich. With an all-star cast including Robert Pattinson, Rihanna, Josh O’Connor and LaKeith Stanfield, the ad captures a familiar festive warmth, supported by its seemingly hand-illustrated animation.

watching "christian dior's night in color" and.... am i the only one noticing this is ai? https://t.co/7QEHjFtAMMNovember 4, 2026

But soon eagle-eyed viewers spotted some subtle discrepancies. Strange warped lettering, a lazy eye here, a misplaced phalange there – all signs seemed to point to AI slop.

But it was not the alleged AI that irked critics so much; it was the deception. A 'behind-the-scenes' look at production shows only sketches, and as the end credits roll, a short list of animators are listed with no mention of AI, (despite evidence suggesting otherwise). Paired with Chomet's creative legacy, Dior's intentions become clear – it wants you to believe this is 100% human animation.

Comments were suitably heated, with one X user writing "Your budget couldn't fit in an actual animation studio? You had to use AI? Disappointing." Another added "After the Prada commercial, its so easy to see its AI. Its so sad to see many good artists put their names behind this," while another added, "Oh Dior can rot in a ditch for not disclosing they resorted to ai-slop. All these 'pretty' visuals mean nothing if no artist animated them."

During this year's Christmas ad season, human-made craft has become synonymous with nostalgia and luxury – praiseworthy traits that earn brand's the audience seal of approval. Dior's sly attempt to capitalise on this trend has only peeved critics further, proving that even the most subtle AI blemishes will be called out. While Dior's AI augmentation is merely speculation, the brand's lack of transparency only aids in audience mistrust and frustration.

ICA Miami selects architect to renovate recently acquired building

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Rendering of ICA Miami’s new building Courtesy Gensler

The Institute of Contemporary Art, Miami (ICA Miami) has appointed the global architecture firm Gensler to renovate a 30,000-sq.-ft building the museum acquired in 2024, doubling its exhibition and public-programming space.

Steps away from its current venue in Miami’s Design District, the new wing is scheduled to open in late 2027—as the museum marks its tenth anniversary at its current location. Focused on expanding the free museum’s community outreach, the project will transform the ICA into a multi-site complex and house the first permanent display of its collection.

The museum’s new building formerly housed the De la Cruz Collection, a private museum that closed after the 2024 death of its co-founder Rosa de la Cruz. Since its $25m acquisition later that year, the site has mainly hosted the ICA’s educational programmes. It will close once construction begins. The renovation is part of a $40m capital campaign, supported entirely by private funds.

The new design will adapt the existing structure. “The building was developed to house a contemporary art collection,” Alex Gartenfeld, the ICA’s director and chief curator, tells The Art Newspaper. “But with the spaces we are constructing, we intend to enhance the museum’s story-driven narrative approach to the collection and exhibitions.”

Rendering of ICA Miami’s new building Courtesy Gensler

Eight new galleries will occupy the building’s first and second floors. Among them will be the museum’s first dedicated spaces for its growing permanent collection of more than 2,000 works.

“The programme between the two buildings is blended, but we will now have two full floors dedicated to our permanent collection,” Gartenfeld says. “Around every six months, we will refresh the collection display to reflect the collection’s emphasis and recent acquisitions.”

The entire third floor will be dedicated to public programming, including talks, symposia, lectures and performances. “Beyond adding galleries, this renovation will strengthen the ICA’s ability to support artists, expand learning and create a platform for exchange and belonging in one of the world’s most dynamic cultural cities,” says Bevin Savage Yamazaki, Gensler’s global culture and museums leader.

A reimagined façade with transparent components is key to the design. Gartenfeld says the façade aims to provide a connection between street-level traffic and the museum itself.

The renovations focus on visitor experience and accessibility as well. The lobby will be redesigned. And reconfigured outdoor areas will include social spaces, a sculpture deck and an expanded café. “We are aiming to create a more collective experience,” Gartenfeld says.

The museum expanded its exhibition space and reorganised its permanent collection for the first time in almost 100 years—with a special focus on Native American art

Several institutional construction projects have already opened this year, with more in the pipeline

Museum renovation showcases creative and sustainable design ideas to help transform contemporary life
